## How to read this file

* **Parts** are `workflows/12`'s part keys, by the canon's home rules: `model` §2; `kernel` §3; `pipeline` §4, §10,
  PART II, PART III; `determinism` §5–§7; `rendering` §8, §9, §11, §12; `scope` the title block and one-page summary,
  §0, §1, §13–§17, the Delegated decisions, the Judgment-calls index, *Staged by the owner's roadmap*, Appendix A.
* **State** judges the live design against the item's ruling and CONFIRMED editor change: SATISFIED (nothing to change),
  PARTIAL (quote what stands and what is missing; "citation" = the mechanism is already ours and only the reference claim
  beside it is wrong) or ABSENT.
* **Home** is the one section that states the mechanism (canon R1–R13); **referencing** lists the other sections —
  cross-part first, then same-part — with what each must say. Same-part places are the home seat's own work unless the
  subsection-owner table (§C.2) gives them to another seat of that part.
* **Gate** per item: which parts are CONFIRMED (apply) and which are HOLD — PLAUSIBLE (do not apply; §C.4 lists them all).
* **Evidence depth** per item: ✓ re-derived by the critic, ⚙ executed against captures, R reader-only — all are
  CONFIRMED under the amendment file's gate; the depth is what Fable's retrovalidation should look at first.
* **Owner-rule items (63–72, and 20 (h) routed as item 20)** have no evidence gate: their Gate reads `owner rule`, State judges the design against
  the item's lettered interpretation, and each section adds a **fidelity** line (the owner's words against that
  interpretation) and the **rulings** its home editor must take and record (§C.3 (10)). Item 68 is the one owner-rule item
  whose emulation note states facts read in the references' code: those facts take the file's gates (§B item 68, §C.4). Item 69's numbers are the owner-released corpus palette bands, taken from the item's
  text only; 20 (h) (v) is the program's proposal whose key law belongs to `docs/AMENDMENTS-2026-09-27d.md` item 74.
* **Items 73–88 (file d) — program candidates, not owner rules.** Their gate is file d's own: every SettlementForge
  fact is CONFIRMED by the surveys and, where a V-id is given, re-derived by `research/sf/mapping-verify.md` (its
  CORRECTED verdicts bind and are already carried by the items, §C.9.4); every shape, threshold and table row is the
  program's own proposal, marked PLAUSIBLE — applied as the design's vetoable decision and recorded as a judgment row,
  never held. No part of 73–88 is marked HOLD, so nothing is held (§C.9.4). SettlementForge's field names and values
  are quoted freely (the owner's own project); no SettlementForge code, file path or line number enters the design.
  The owner rulings bind: Q1 and Q2 (J86) and Q3–Q6 (J80), each placed at its item (§C.9.6). The **owner words** of a
  d item are the owner rule it extends (20 (h), J77) and the rulings it carries.
* **Item 101 (file e) — an OWNER RULE**, routed as items 63–72 were: `owner rule` gate, a fidelity line, the rulings
  its home editor records as `J-A101.⟨n⟩`; (f′) supersedes (f)'s wording and adds R13–R19 and the first-five-minutes
  bar; its HOMES paragraphs are the program's placement, checked here against the canon (§B item 101).

staged evolution and decline engine, floating islands, sewers, underground neighbourhoods or SettlementForge inputs;

sewer network, a few closure-like edits, the ruin ladder — and none finds its mechanism); ABSENT 8. Homes: pipeline 21

require/forbid); II.6 and II.7 now take edits.

owner-rule item with a held part — its emulation note's absence claim about MFCG (§C.4).

## B. The items

Each section: **ruling and gate** · **state** (quoted, with line numbers) · **home** · **referencing** (cross-part, then
same-part) · **staged** · **decisions touched** (quoted) · **family** · editor notes (owner duties from §C.2, identifier
hazards from §C.5). Provenance tags follow §C.3.

### Item 32 — the code-reading provenance sentence (§17.1)

* **Owner words** (2026-09-27, quoted in `docs/AMENDMENTS-2026-09-25b.md` item 32): "oh read them. don't hesitate. we
  captured it, we read it, unless we directly copy their code line by line, that is copyright. but to read them and to
  understand their logic, that is another thing entirely."

### Item 65 — OWNER RULE: the underground layer as a family of systems, and the undercity (II.7; §2.1–§2.8; §3.4; §8.4; §9.1; §1.1)

* **Owner words** (2026-09-27, item 65; PROGRESS J55, J57, J58), as normalised in the amendments file's copy (the owner's
  message is the record): "one last thing and all of this is for the underground layer. I think the central piece is a
  derived sewer system as you have already built out. But other additions to keep in mind is catacombs (underneath
  cemeteries or churches for example), cavernous tunnels, sewer dwellings and networks, and an undercity. Those are all
  options that exist in the same level with varying degrees of connections and access between them (not always). The
  hardest part for me to conceptualize is an undercity. we already have dwellings or underbuildings that are adjacent to
  the sewer walls. those are more standoffish. But what about an undercity, what would that require? In my mind, it is
  still part of a sewer network or cavern or catacomb, but it is a large space or network where there is a collection of
  buildings/dwellings to be a microcosm of a city but of the riffraff, criminal, and less savory types or more desperate
  go." Then: "i would say that the undercity is at most 1-3 neighborhoods to keep it simple and consistent"; "while it is
  called an undercity, it is simply the less than savory shadow and extension of the full settlement, not independent of
  it".
* **Ruling and gate.** OWNER RULE; (a)–(g), with (c)'s bound and (c′), are the program's vetoable interpretation and the
  answer to the owner's question "what would that require?" — (b)'s four requirements. No evidence gate; nothing held.
  **Fidelity:** "the central piece is a derived sewer system" = the network of II.7.1–II.7.4 unchanged at the centre;
  "catacombs … cavernous tunnels, sewer dwellings and networks, and an undercity … in the same level" = (a)'s systems on
  one layer; "varying degrees of connections and access between them (not always)" = (d)'s typed links, never guaranteed;
  "still part of a sewer network or cavern or catacomb, but … a large space … a microcosm of a city" = (b) (1)–(4), the
  void and its town-like parts; "at most 1-3 neighborhoods" = (c)'s hard bound; "the less than savory shadow and
  extension of the full settlement, not independent of it" = (c′). Two additions are the program's own, flagged for
  vetting: QUARRIES and MINE VOIDS (the town's stone came from somewhere) and a BURIED OLD TOWN as a void source (from the
  evolution log) — both historically attested (Paris's quarries, bridge and street vaults, buried street levels), both
  realism.

* **Rulings the home editor must take and record** (recommendations PLAUSIBLE):
  1. **Crossing at depth vs one arena (J-A12.45 "one underground arena; depth per entity").** Systems that "pass over and

     system on the `underground` layer (PlatKey parts; the sewer keeps part 0), each system's topology separate, links as
     cross-arena records like `AccessPoint`, the layer drawn as one by depth band — J-A12.45 amended for systems (depth per
     entity still holds inside a system). Needs the kernel seat's confirmation of J-A12.32's reading (a transaction

     galleries, walkways and main chamber (its streets, (b) (2)), which U2's letter ("no `way` chain and no `alley` face on
     the underground arena") forbids if the undercity shares the sewer's arena. Recommended: U1–U3 stated as the sewer

  3. **Realism or toggle (D-3).** Catacombs, quarries, caverns, vaults and a buried old town are historically attested:
     existence by rule (realism), hidden with the underground layer. The undercity at the owner's "microcosm" scale reads as
     fantasy: recommended existence by rule at a modest scale in the `historical` preset (vault and cellar warrens, a
     buried street) and the full microcosm with the `navigable` preset (D-3's toggle) — the scope seat records D-3's clause.
  4. **The quarter tree.** (c′) names undercity neighbourhoods as the underside of their parent quarter, where II.7.3 keeps
     the sewer pseudo-district in "a family of its own". Recommended: the undercity joins the parent quarter's node; the
     sewer pseudo-district stays as is.
  5. **The buried old town.** A conversion kind (for example `raiseGrade { region; raiseCm }`) re-bases a region's ground
     (S1's terrain operator) and keeps the old street level's ways, plots and building shells as buried records on the
     underground layer; the evolution log supplies it (epoch history), a user by hand. Its §4.11 union line is folded by

### Item 66 — OWNER RULE: an access edit generates everything it needs — the closure rule (§7.3; §7.1; §7.4; §2.6; II.7)

* **Owner words** (2026-09-27, item 66; PROGRESS J56): "but make it so that if a user edits a building to have an access to
  the underground, it generates all the necessary pieces".
* **Ruling and gate.** OWNER RULE; the item's (a)–(e) are the program's vetoable interpretation. No evidence gate; nothing
  held. **Fidelity:** "if a user edits a building to have an access" = one user edit, `addUndergroundAccess` (a);
  "it generates all the necessary pieces" = the closure (a) (1)–(7) — basement, target, connector, a minimal private
  underground where none exists, the access point, the plan reservation, the links. (b) seed and locality, (c) reversal
  and ownership and (d) generality apply standing rules (§6's seed rules, §7's locality, undo and everything editable);
  (d)'s generalisation ("any edit that implies missing prerequisites") is the program's and is the reason the rule homes in
  §7, not in II.7. Owner fidelity on the edge case: "the user's request overrides the probability, not the physics" — a
  cellar the terrain forbids is refused WITH the reason or replaced by the nearest lawful form, never silently (J-A29.22's
  water-table gate stands).
* **State: PARTIAL.** Instances of a closure exist, with no general rule: `drawFootprint` on unplotted land "creates a plot
  around it with access to the nearest way, or returns a conflict if none is reachable" (l. 6217–6219); the tree brush
  "where no green entity exists first logs a `drawGreen` … to host them" (l. 6243–6245 — a prerequisite as a SEPARATE
  event, not one); `placeAt` joins plots and mutates the buildings (§7.7 (4), l. 7029–7032); the chained operators of §7.3
  item 3 run whole on the overlay (l. 6287). ABSENT: the general closure rule (one event, one transaction, dependency
  order, the record of created pieces, exact undo, deletion of unshared pieces only); `addUndergroundAccess`; the
  basement's requested mode and lawful-form ladder; the dug connector and the minimal private underground; the ownership
  of shared pieces. Today an access point is added "through `setAttr` on the `AccessPoint` record" (l. 6273), which would
  leave a building without a basement holding a stair to nowhere.
* **Home:** determinism · **§7.3** (seat E) — a new numbered item, **"9. Closure edits — an edit generates the prerequisites
  it implies (item 66)"**, and a row in item 3's operator table ("an edit whose prerequisites are missing | the closure,
  expanded in dependency order on the overlay | … "): an edit with missing prerequisites is expanded at dry run into its
  CLOSURE — the minimal set of pieces in dependency order — applied as ONE event and ONE transaction (§3.3's batches, one
  arena per batch); every piece draws from the edited entity's and the target's lineage streams and is minted from the
  event's `seq` (J-A18.43's pattern), so the same edit on the same seed gives the same pieces; the effective scope is the
  closure's pieces and their clear regions, byte identity holding outside it (the oracle, item 7); the event records which
  pieces it created (the closure record); `undo` removes exactly those; a later deletion of the edited thing removes only
  the created pieces nothing else uses (a connector another access now shares stays), and a basement the closure created
  reverts to the derived state unless the user kept it; the closure creates map pieces, never dossier facts (item 20);
  (d) other instances — a clandestine front requiring a concealed door (item 63), an undercity entrance requiring a
  lifeline (item 65) — use the same rule. `addUndergroundAccess(building, target?)` is its first, staged instance.

### Item 67 — OWNER RULE: transitions — buildings and plots shown mid-change on a weekly clock (§4.11; §2.5–§2.9; §8.2; §8.5)

* **Owner words** (2026-09-27, item 67; PROGRESS J59): "now something else I want us to figure out and consider...
  transitions.... so hear me out, when a building gets torn down, ruined, being built, being expanded, Lots need to merge
  or separate. But depending on where things are in the process of being built or ruined, we should consider things being
  roofless or other visuals that they are in a transition state. note that our simulator advances by units of one week. so
  if it takes two weeks or a month to build a full house, then during that time, we should show stages of it being built.
  What do you think those stages should be?"
* **Ruling and gate.** OWNER RULE; the item's (a)–(k) are the program's vetoable interpretation and its ANSWER to the
  owner's question (the stages of (c), (e) and (f)). No evidence gate; nothing held. **Fidelity:** "torn down, ruined,
  being built, being expanded" = the four transition families (e), (f), (c), (d); "Lots need to merge or separate" = (g);
  "roofless or other visuals that they are in a transition state" = the drawn stages and their rows (c)–(h); "our simulator
  advances by units of one week … two weeks or a month to build a full house … show stages of it being built" = (a)'s
  derived stage at any week and (b)'s durations (a small timber house 2–6 weeks). (i) restraint, (j) stale markers and (k)
  bars apply standing rules (items 8, 28, 31). "Our simulator" is read as the clock that drives the map's history — the
  host's (SettlementForge's) or the staged evolution engine's epochs, as amendments-b item 31 (a) calls it "the
  simulator's clock or a dossier change" — NOT `@plat/sim`'s `time` view key, which "never changes the model or logs an
  event" (§10.3 l. 8608–8611). The editor records this reading.
* **State: PARTIAL.** Stands: the ruin ladder's coarse states — §2.8 l. 1603–1618 "occupied → vacant (`abandoned`) →
  derelict (`decayed`, roofless: `RoofSpec.kind 'none'`) → ruin (`ruined`) → robbed or cleared (`cleared` …) → reused";
  `RoofSpec.kind 'none'` l. 972 "a ruined building's roofless state"; §4.11's ladders and decline table with dwell bands in
  YEARS (l. 5121 "vacant → derelict [10, 30]; derelict → ruin [20, 60]"); `Building.condition` kept → worn → neglected
  (l. 965); §8.2's `states` rows for abandoned, decayed, ruined, cleared, reused and worn (l. 7446–7465); the epoch-spread
  mutation (l. 5071–5075 — item 18's steps spread over the ladder's durations, the extension's nearest analogue); the
  epoch as a logged event with `EpochInputs { years; … }` (l. 5038); the churn cap "per decade, scaled by the epoch's
  `years`" (l. 4835–4838). ABSENT: any record of a change in progress (a `Works`); the week as the clock's unit and an
  absolute week; construction stages; demolition as a timed process (`demolish` is one op, l. 4914, and the building
  becomes a tombstone); the stage derived from a record at a week; the ruin's intermediate drawn stages; plot boundaries
  that change on the ground in step with the work; a scrub key; heights and roof at a stage; the season row; the
  restraint share; the bars.
* **Home:** pipeline · **§4.11** (seat T, which owns §4.11's REPLACE, §C.2) — a new paragraph, **"Works — every physical
  change as a timed project on the weekly clock (item 67)"**, with its table and its kinds: (a) every physical change —
  build, extend, demolish, repair, convert, decay, clear — is a `Works` record (kind, target building, wing, plot or plots,
  start week, duration in weeks, stage schedule, cause); the drawn state at any week is DERIVED (elapsed ÷ duration →
  stage), never stored per week; decay is a slow Works driven by vacancy and time; fire an instant event followed by a
  clearance or repair Works; (b) `WorksTable` (in `@plat/tables`, §7.6 — rows by class × size × material × prosperity ×
  season, the item's defaults: a hovel or shed 1–2 weeks, a small timber house 2–6 weeks, a town house 2–4 months, a stone
  house 4–12 months, a manor 1–3 years, a church a decade or more, a cathedral generations built part by part; demolition
  much faster; decay years to decades, faster for timber and thatch); a stage shorter than a week is skipped in the drawing;
  (c) the construction stages — staked, ground broken, foundations, walls rising, roof framed, roofed, finished — as stage
  fractions per class row, the building `built` and occupied at FINISHED; (d) extension — the building in use, the new wing
  through (c), the old roof opened at roof-framed where the two join, the joint wall opened and the wings united at
  finished (item 18's add step, spread in time); (e) demolition — emptied, stripped, roofless shell, rubble, cleared;
  (f) ruin — abandoned, decaying, roofless, collapsed (stone to the salvage ledger), overgrown; fire jumps to a charred
  roofless shell in one week; repair runs the ruin stages backwards under scaffolding; (g) plots in transition (the
  ownership decided at the start; the ground follows the work); (i) restraint — the share of buildings in works follows
  the settlement's growth or decline rate, the parity presets none; (j) a stage change marks the building's rungs stale;
  the clock in `EpochInputs` (below). With no epoch nothing here runs (item 23: neither reference has time).
* **Rulings the home editor must take and record** (recommendations PLAUSIBLE):
  1. **The clock's unit.** `EpochInputs { years }` (l. 5038) has no week. Recommended: `EpochInputs.weeks` (≥ 1; the host's
     tick is one week) and the document's absolute week (the sum), `years` derived through the calendar's weeks per year.
  2. **Per-epoch chances under a weekly clock.** Every row stated "per epoch" (the terrain-water trigger rows, l. 5213–5221:
     "0.02 per epoch", "0.03 per epoch", "0.01 per epoch"; II.6's island slots re-drawn "each epoch") would fire 52 times as
     often at weekly epochs. Recommended: rows stated per year and applied as `1 − (1 − p)^years` of the epoch's span;
     dwell bands in years stay; the churn caps already scale by `years`. A correction the weekly clock forces on §4.11 and
     II.6 (seat T writes §4.11's; seat D folds II.6's slot clause).
  3. **Existence during works.** The item has the building become `built` at FINISHED. Recommended: no new `Existence`
     value — the target carries a live `works` link, its stage derived; construction's occupants move in by the Works'
     completion event; the item's five ruin stages are the drawn stages of the existing ladder (ABANDONED = `abandoned`;
     DECAYING = the stage of the decay Works toward `decayed`; ROOFLESS = `decayed`; COLLAPSED = `ruined`; OVERGROWN =
     `cleared` or `reused`) — J-A8.46 stands. The model editor states it.
  4. **Demolition by hand.** An engine `demolish` becomes a Works whose last stage makes the tombstone (J-A8.42 unchanged
     at the end); a user's `convert` with `demolish` stays instant unless the edit schedules it (`startWorks`), so the base
     keeps its edit semantics.
  5. **Material in the durations.** The owner: "different materials for construction is only represented by a change in
     texture or color. It has nothing to do with the shape"; §10.2 "No row names a material"; J-A31.42 replaceability
     "never keyed by material". A duration is time, not shape: recommended ADMIT material as a duration row of `WorksTable`
     (realism — stone builds and decays slower), stated beside J-A31.42, which stays for replaceability, and never as a
     shape input.
  6. **Plots in transition.** Recommended: the plat's L3 rings change when the Works starts (ownership, the item's (g)); the
     old line's `PlotBound` treatment persists as a pending-removal bound until the ground-broken or rubble stage, and the
     new line appears as stakes at staking and as a fence or wall at walls-rising; pending lines dashed. Item 31's `subdivide`
     and `amalgamate` run through the same stages.
  7. **Scrubbing.** "Any week can be shown, replayed or scrubbed" — recommended: a per-viewer view key `week` (§2.9) that
     draws every live or recently finished Works at week w over the current model (a finished Works kept for a retention
     span); the full history at an earlier week is the log's replay (§7.4). Outside every digest.
  8. **The season.** Masonry pausing in winter needs a calendar in the base (§10.3's calendar is the module's):
     recommended `WorksTable.calendar` (weeks per year, the paused weeks by region and era), which the module reads too.
* **Referencing.** Cross-part:

### Item 68 — OWNER RULE: light — day and night, the cursor sun, point lights, gradient shadows that respect height (§8.4; §8.2; §2.7; §4.7; §1.7; D-1)

* **Owner words** (2026-09-27, item 68; spelling normalised in the amendments file only): "next thing, daytime vs night
  time view, lights such as torches or magical lights vs the sun, global light as the sun versus radial light sources from
  torches/magical lights, and shadows that correspond with shape and height. 1. a daytime view is the default. the global
  light is the cursor. every shadow moves in accordance with wherever the global light (the cursor) is radiating against
  them 2. a night time view with appropriately spaced and partitioned torches or magical lights. the moon is a global light
  but much softer than the sun. all of these does somewhat of the opposite, it brightens a shadow ridden area. 3. shadows
  are gradients affected by light sources, during the day time it primarily comes from the sun. During the night time it
  softens according to multiple light sources. There is not just one shade for shadow, and we consider shadows the absence
  of light which means that a torch radiates in all direction but if there is an object between multiple torches where
  every side is lit, it would have no shadow then. and light is also reflected in the dark meaning that indirect light
  sources also help to illuminate whether from a torch or the moon. 4. shadows of buildings also have to take into account
  the height of a building it may be cast on. We only see the rooftops, however, if there was a wizard tower tall and
  giant, a small hovel's shadow if the shadow is cast on the tower, does not produce one on its roof. But the tower can on
  the hovel subtracting the distance of the shadow on the ground and the height of the hovel, traveling further if
  appropriate beyond the hovel onto the ground or next building. shadows also take into account the height of their light
  sources and distance. 5. Torches have optimal distances and gradients of effectiveness as well as light gets dispersed
  over distance." Confirmation: "and the cursor moves the global light which creates different angles for every shadow".
  Underground: "the undercity and near dwellings underground has to have a lot of light sources. they don't have the sun
  or moon". Standing rule (2026-09-25, `docs/OWNER-INSTRUCTIONS-2026-09-25.md`): "add an element of height … eventually,
  I want to add shadows and they would use that height number."
* **Ruling and gate.** OWNER RULE; the item's (a)–(h) are the program's vetoable interpretation, and the owner's words
  carry no evidence gate. The **emulation note (g)** is different: it states facts read in the references' compiled code,
  so the file's three gates bind it. **CONFIRMED** (reader grade): Fantasy Manor 1.0.0 build 890 shades each roof facet
  from one light with a diffuse and a specular term and draws its shadow as the building's silhouette swept along an
  offset in one tint, multiplied (`code-manor.md` §2.10–§2.14, §6 rows 16–17; replayed); the FTG client of the 2026-09-25
  capture lights its scene with one ambient and one directional light, the sun on an hour path and, at night, the same
  path carrying a low-intensity moon; the directional light — and so every shadow — exists only in clear or snow weather;
  shadows come from a shadow map of invisible extrusions of every footprint (walls and fences by edge type); window lights
  come from the lit set its server returns, drawn at a per-building anchor (`code-ftg-client.md` §2.2, §2.5.2, §2.5.5,
  §2.5.6; rows C5, C13, C19, C22). **HOLD — PLAUSIBLE:** "MFCG draws no cast shadows" is an absence inferred from the full
  read of MFCG 12710731, not a read fact — the editor writes the `MFCG` preset's behaviour as ours (its raised under-copy,
  K06 and item 54's P15, and no cast shadow) and makes no claim about the reference (§C.4). **Not in the item, CONFIRMED:**
  NG 1.2.2 draws a prism shadow per wing (the footprint offset against the light by height × a length factor, one quad per
  away-facing edge, multiplied at an alpha), tree and pin shadows likewise, and at night an ambience wash with a radial
  glow on each building by a chance (`code-neighbourhood.md` §2.11) — `hatched` already emulates the prism pass (§8.2's
  presets row; §15.1 E5–E6). **Nothing may be written** about VG 1.6.9's shadow and night-light layers (search-only, critic
  G3) or Dwellings' shading gradients beyond J-A28.32's room lights (skimmed view classes, critic R11). **R5:** the
  readings' constants (FM's light-angle band, NG's shadow alpha, length and direction, FTG's moon intensity and sun-height
  steps) may enter only as reference observations attached to a parity-preset cell, with a P-id — and none exists:
  proposed **P22** (the FTG client's light model), **P23** (NG 1.2.2's prism shadow and night glow), **P24** (FM build
  890's facet shading and swept shadow), which the pass's manager appends to `research/findings/code-impact.md` §(d)
  before a design cell cites them (§C.3 (3)). Build naming (gate (b)) applies to every "the reference does X" sentence of
  the preset cells; no reference class, method, parameter or style-key name enters (gate (c)).
* **Fidelity.** Point 1 ("a daytime view is the default. the global light is the cursor. every shadow moves …") and the
  confirmation ("different angles for every shadow") = (c) and (c′): the sun stands at a finite height H above the
  pointer's ground point, every caster's shadow points away from it, a radial light rather than a parallel sun that turns.
  Point 2 ("a night time view with appropriately spaced and partitioned torches or magical lights. the moon is a global
  light but much softer") = (d): a second, softer global light and point lights placed by rule at a spacing derived from
  their radius and height; "partitioned" is read as distributed by street class, district and prosperity band through the
  rule rows (the route's reading; the editor records it); "it brightens a shadow ridden area" = (a)'s additive sum. Point 3
  ("gradients … not just one shade … every side is lit … no shadow … indirect") = (a): penumbrae from the source's size
  and distance, cancellation where other sources reach, an indirect term and ambient occlusion. Point 4 (the tower on the
  hovel "subtracting the distance … and the height of the hovel, traveling further … beyond"; the hovel's shadow never on
  the tower's roof; "the height of their light sources and distance") = (b) and (c′)'s similar-triangles law. Point 5
  ("optimal distances and gradients of effectiveness … dispersed over distance") = (d)'s inverse-square falloff with a
  smooth cutoff at an effective radius and the spacing rule. The underground words = (d′). The one place the
  interpretation is narrower than the words is (c)'s "INTERPRETATION TO CONFIRM" (the ink default without cast shadows):
  ruling 2.
* **State: PARTIAL.** Stands — §8.4 l. 7639–7642 "The **raised under-copy** (`raised`) is MFCG's Raised … ON by default
  in `ink`, not a shadow, not height-dependent. **Projected shadows** (`ShadowPass(layer, sun)`) are height-derived and OFF
  by default (owner: shadows optional)"; the casters, l. 7643–7651 "Every caster projects along the sun vector from its
  base elevation (`baseCm`, FTG-13) onto the ground … `drop` offsets each caster's footprint by height × cot(altitude) …
  `prism` draws the swept prism"; the sun, l. 7652–7660 "`SunSpec = { azimuthCdeg; altitudeCdeg; colour: Color; intensity:
  number; mode: 'fixed'|'path'; latitudeDeg: number }`; `fixed` by default (azimuth 315°, altitude 45°: light from the upper
  left) … Below −6° the sheet switches to its night variant (`night` roles; `lights` glow instances from the module) …
  Shadows are vector: no shadow-resolution setting"; the light step and its light pass (l. 7670–7680, J-R54); the heights
  (§2.6 l. 943 `Heights { baseCm; floors; floorsBelow?; eaveCm; ridgeCm; towerCm? }`, l. 972 `RoofSpec.pitch`) and the
  display list's `heightCm` and `baseCm` columns and terrain `field` marks (§8.1 l. 7161, 7195); the roles `shadow`,
  `ambience`, `light`, `lights` (§8.2 l. 7280–7281); `lit` "one directional light from the sheet's sun … soft shadows are
  `shadowPass` polygons with `penumbraPx` … vector, no bitmap filter … `lit` does not draw per-pixel normal-map relief on
  roofs and walls" (l. 7541–7548, J-R4); the `night` ink variant "`lights` role with glow instances" (l. 7535); "overcast
  turns the projected shadow pass off" (l. 7424 — already FTG's weather rule); `Pin.kind` `'lamp'`, `'campfire'`,
  `'fire_pit'` (§2.7 l. 1485); the view keys time, season and weather (§2.9 l. 1968–1972) and the module's
  `occupancy(dayType, hour)` and `time` key (§10.3 l. 8604–8611); dd2vtt's "`lights` — the module's light sources in the
  scope, none otherwise" (§11.1 l. 8787–8788); §15.1 D5 "day/night, weather, seasons, window lights | §8.4 sun vector +
  shadow hook; `night` preset with the `lights` role; lights from the module | BASE (hook) + module" (l. 10662); II.6
  "its shadow falls on the ground through the shadow pass" (l. 11313). ABSENT (searched over the whole document, 0 hits
  unless noted): a cursor sun ("cursor" 0) and any finite-height or radial day light; the moon ("moon" 0); point lights as
  placed entities ("torch", "lantern", "brazier" 0; "light source" 1 — dd2vtt's module lights); an effective radius and an
  inverse-square falloff ("inverse" 0); additive multi-source light in which shadows cancel; indirect light ("indirect"
  0); ambient occlusion (0); any shadow receiver but the ground — a shadow shortened on a lower roof, never climbing a
  taller one; per-pixel or GPU lighting (§1.7 l. 359 rejects "WebGPU-only (too new for the embed target); a WebGL2
  reference backend for `lit` (a second drawing path)"; §8.9 l. 7864–7866 "WebGL2 draws no marks … no look that differs
  by backend (VIS-7)"); a magic circumstance (S0's are "era, government, biome, economy, landscape toggles", l. 3216–3217;
  "magic" appears only in II.6's island supply); lit windows without the module; underground light, light shafts and a
  hand lantern; lighting in the raster and 3-D exports; the bars.
* **Home:** rendering · **§8.4** "Layers, shadows, heights" (seat R's REPLACE; heading kept byte-identical) — the lighting
  model, stated once:
  (a) **the field** — per tile, a height-and-normal buffer rastered in the tile worker from the display list: terrain
  `field` marks; roofs as surfaces from the roof construction (eave, ridge, pitch, the constructed faces — never the
  skeleton directly, so §3.8's readers stay three, J-A28.32, KERNEL-C05); walls, towers, decks and crowns; and, for what a
  single-valued field cannot hold, **occluder slabs** with a top and an underside (a sky island, a bridge deck, the spanning
  wing over a passage that amendments-b's repair round adds as a caster, a crown over ground) — ruling 3;
  (b) **the sources** — the sun and the moon (global), the placed `LightSource` records (§2.7), the derived sources (lit
  windows by §10.3's rule; the plan rung's room lights at the building band; the underground's light shafts), the hand
  lantern (viewer-side, never a record);
  (c) **brightness** at a point = Σ over sources of intensity × falloff(distance) × facing × visibility, + ambient
  (sky by day; moon and sky at night; near zero underground) + indirect − ambient occlusion; falloff inverse-square with a
  smooth cutoff at the source's `radiusCm`; visibility by marching the field and the slabs from the point toward the source
  at the source's height; penumbra from the source's size and distance; a shadow is one source's absence, so several
  lights cancel it; indirect a blurred spill of the direct light; occlusion from the field (narrow alleys, courts, the foot
  of tall walls);
  (d) **the cursor sun** — `SunSpec.mode` gains `'cursor'`: the sun at H above the pointer's ground point, a caster of
  height h at horizontal distance d throwing its tip to d · h ÷ (H − h) beyond it, capped at the view's edge when h ≥ H;
  H = `sunHeight` × the view's extent; `'fixed'` (the sheet's 315° / 45°) wherever there is no pointer — first paint,
  touch, a still image, every export; `'path'` kept; the moon the same model at the night view's intensity and colour (a
  phase row);
  (e) **day and night** — the `light` view key (§2.9); the night view is every family's night variant; how much light a
  night has is the placement rows' (§4.7) and the era, magic and prosperity rows';
  (f) **the light pass** — a post operation over the base raster (slots 1–20), before labels and furniture (slots
  21–25); a **CPU reference** in the tile worker (PNG, print, stills, goldens, tests) and a **GPU implementation** (WebGL2,
  WebGPU where available) for the interactive cursor, held to the reference by a tolerance; static lights baked once per
  (tile, zoom stop, lights revision, style) into a light map with their shadows, culled per tile; only the sun or moon
  term re-evaluated per frame;
  (g) **the fallback ladder** — GPU field → a half-resolution light buffer → today's vector `ShadowPass` with glow sprites
  (kept also for vector exports and for any preset that asks for it) — with its measurement and pre-decided fallback;
  (h) **K06 unchanged** — the raised under-copy is not a shadow and stays ON in `ink`;
  (i) **the parity presets** — `MFCG`: the raised under-copy only, no cast shadow, no night; `FTG`: the fixed or path sun,
  the low moon on the path at night, shadows only in clear or snow weather, window lights at each lit building's main
  entrance (item 50: FTG's "entrance" is this light's anchor); `hatched`: NG 1.2.2's prism pass; the stage-3 building band:
  FM's facet shading — each cell with `provenance` `ref:⟨build⟩ ⟨behaviour⟩ (code)` and its P-id, every row of ours with
  the `extension` marker (J-A23.71).
* **Rulings the home editor must take and record** (recommendations PLAUSIBLE; `J-A68.8⟨n⟩` for §8 rulings, `J-A68.0⟨n⟩`
  for the scope clauses):
  1. **One drawing path (J-R4, D-1, VIS-7, §1.7, §8.9).** Per-pixel GPU lighting meets the design's central rendering
     decision: Canvas2D the reference backend of every preset, "no look that differs by backend", a WebGL2 reference
     backend for `lit` rejected. Recommended: the lighting is a post operation over the Canvas2D base raster, never a second
     path for marks; its CPU implementation is the reference (exports, goldens, §6.5's test); the GPU is its accelerator
     for the moving cursor, held to the reference by §13.4's tolerance; D-1's rejection stands as written; J-R4 is restated
     by name (its "normal-mapped relief is the only loss" becomes per-pixel light through the post operation); §8.9's
     "WebGL2 draws no marks" stays true, the GPU now also running the light pass.
  2. **The default (D-19, §1.2, K06; the item's "INTERPRETATION TO CONFIRM").** The owner's point 1 describes the
     default view's shadows moving with the cursor; D-19 reads "shadows off by default with a fixed sun (§8.4)"; §1.2
     l. 259–260 "the `ink` style with MFCG's raised under-copy on and the projected shadow off"; J10 and D-1 make MFCG's ink
     the default look. Recommended (the item's own reading): the day view and the cursor sun are the defaults in every
     family (D-19's "fixed sun" gives way to `cursor`, the fixed sun kept where there is no pointer); cast shadows ON by
     default in `lit` and `hatched`; `ink` keeps its raised under-copy with cast shadows one switch away, so the default ink
     map still reads as MFCG's (item 23). Fidelity risk: the owner opening the default ink map sees no cursor shadow — so
     the scope seat puts this reading to the owner in plain words (item 68's §0.2 landing row; the validator's owner
     summary), as the item asks. The alternative — cast shadows on in `ink` too, at the ink shadow alpha, only the `MFCG`
     preset without them — is the fidelity-first reading; the editor records whichever it takes, the other as the rejected
     option. The first paint's cost (§12.5; §1.2 "the fast path never pays for … projected shadows") follows the ruling.
  3. **Overhangs.** A height field holds one surface per point; islands (II.6), bridge decks (§8.4's deck casters), the
     spanning wing over a passage and crowns have ground beneath them. Recommended: occluder slabs beside the field, marched
     with it, so the ground under a deck is shaded from above yet lit from the side and an island's shadow falls from its
     underside.
  4. **The radial sun and zoom.** H proportional to the view's extent makes a zoom change on-screen shadow lengths.
     Recommended: accepted as view-derived (nothing stored); `sunHeight` at its top gives the near-parallel sun, whose
     lengths do not depend on zoom — the only mode for exports and stills.
  5. **Lights as records.** Recommended: placed lights are `LightSource` records (model digest, editable, addressed by host
     lineage); windows and the plan rung's room lights are derived per view (J-A28.32's room lights stay the plan rung's,
     fed to the pass at the building band); the hand lantern is viewer state.
  6. **How much light (D-3; items 23, 27).** Recommended: the target minimum brightness per street class as band rows
     interpolated along the prosperity value (a gradient, never a switch); the era row for public-lighting density
     (sparse in the medieval default); `magic` a new S0 circumstance, `none` by default — magical lights a named fantasy
     toggle under D-3 — while torches, lanterns and braziers are realism and the night view is a view choice; D-3's "the
     `night` preset's lights" is restated.
  7. **Determinism of lighting ((f); §6.5; §13.4).** "Lighting identical for the same model, lights and view":
     recommended byte-identity for the CPU reference on the five engines, the GPU within the tolerance, flicker a pure
     function of the light's address hash and the time.
  8. **Double lighting and legibility (J-R54, J-R21, §9.4).** With the pass on, `faceLight`, the two-tone roof,
     `hillshade` and crown shading emit albedo and normals and the pass lights them once; the light step stays for the
     fallback. Labels, halos and furniture are composed after the pass so the label floor holds at night — which splits
     J-R21's "base and labels composed into one bitmap per tile" while the GPU pass runs (the compositor takes the unlit

* **Owner words** (2026-09-27 ~13:55, item 71; PROGRESS J66): "Also note that regarding bands a prosperous neighborhood if

* **Ruling and gate.** OWNER RULE; (a)–(e) are the program's vetoable interpretation, refining §4.5's bands and §4.7's
  granularity law and placement. No evidence gate; nothing held. **Fidelity:** "a mix where appropriate to its band" =
  (a) class-mix weights per band row, interpolated along the prosperity value ("Keeping with the idea that it is
  gradient"); "and the prosperity of the city" = (b) the town-wide wealth shifts the weights while the band cuts stay the
  town's own; "a very prosperous location may simply have all manors" = (a)'s extreme row (all manors); (c) — prime
  frontages for the larger classes, never salt and pepper — and (d) — households decide who lives there, the map which
  building each gets — are the program's (the owner said nothing about where inside a block the manors stand; realism is
  the tiebreaker, J10 / the design tiebreaker).

### Item 72 — OWNER RULE: what defines a neighbourhood, and names it — character, identities, three dials, the band range, shared buildings (§4.5; §2.5; §10.2; §10.4; §4.11; §7.7)

* **Owner words** (2026-09-27, item 72 and its parts; PROGRESS J67–J71, J73): "By the way, just so you know, prosperity can
  shift for a number of reasons as well as regression and expansion to a number of reasons. Eventually neighborhoods are
  going to be more or less defined by the mix of assigned lots within them and how they correlate to relevant political
  powers as well as the spatial flow of trade both to main streets and the general direction (paired with location of
  walls and gates as gates) where trade is flowing most from, in addition to the economic make up of a settlement that
  can shift, their relationship to the main roads and to the more significant institutions which synergizes with again
  the make up or contributions of assigned lots to institutions within a settlement. This assignment also helps to assign
  names to neighborhoods." Further owner words, quoted in the item at (d′) ("… a red light district might still have a
  blacksmith or an inn … but would also have an increased number of brothels … multiple blacksmiths or multiple
  alchemists or multiple churches as not neighborhood or districts, but something to be dispersed out … if there is a
  neighborhood that has three or more or a lot of churches or it has a grand cathedral which by itself is enough, then
  let's call that neighborhood the religious quarter …"), (d″) ("… two separate dials … it's not just about the number of
  institutions … each church individual value is greater than the lodging …"), (d‴) ("… this neighborhood prosperities
  defines the bounds (floor, and ceiling) of what any building inside of it can be … let's stay with the neighborhood
  bounds until the neighborhood itself upgrades"), (d4) ("If trade is the number one contributor to the economy …
  that is an amplifier to things like markets. If it's taxes and toll roads then any infrastructure becomes amplified more
  … the market share affects their application") and (d5) ("… shared buildings … should not get a double amplification,
  but a partition amplification relative to the strength of both or more powers they contribute to and their economy").
* **Ruling and gate.** OWNER RULE; (a)–(d5) are the program's vetoable interpretation. No evidence gate; nothing held.
  **Fidelity:** "defined by the mix of assigned lots … political powers … the spatial flow of trade … the economic make
  up … their relationship to the main roads and to the more significant institutions … contributions of assigned lots
  to institutions" = (a)'s five components, one each; "prosperity can shift for a number of reasons" = (b) the vector
  and the dials re-read at each epoch from many drivers; "This assignment also helps to assign names" = (c); (d′) — the
  three distributions and the quarter identities with thresholds — quotes the owner's red-light, blacksmith and cathedral
  examples one for one; (d″)'s two dials and "value, not volume" are the owner's; (d‴)'s range is the owner's, and the
  EXCESS as upward pressure on the neighbourhood is the program's reading of "until the neighborhood itself upgrades"
  (the owner stated the bound, not the cause of the upgrade — vetoable, ruling 7); (d4)'s proportionality to market
  share with no rank bonus is the owner's refinement; (d5)'s partition is the owner's. "Eventually" reads as: the
  mechanism is built in the base now and fed by the dossier's facts at the SettlementForge stage (J67's "what do you
  need to do in the interim to prepare"); the base runs it on the blueprint's own circumstances as the dossier's
  stand-in (item 20 (h)).

  into a district by rule; ruling 6); singular or anchor (a cathedral, the palace, the university, the great market —
  sited by their own rules and weighing heavily on their surroundings); **quarter identities** — a table of rows, each a
  significance-weighted count of class families with a threshold, a band condition, a priority and a `singular` flag
  (e.g. the religious quarter: chapels and churches weight 1, a cathedral or great temple weight 3, threshold 3); the
  highest-priority row met gives the district's identity, else its dominant component or a plain residential kind by band;
  dossier-declared kinds win over emergent ones; the S6 kinds (feature-named; J-P1) stay the typing the pipeline reads
  (ruling 2);
  (d″) **two power dials** — every class associated with the power(s) it answers to (a row, dossier-overridable); the
  INSTITUTION dial (every institution of a power rises and falls with that power's standing — condition, upkeep,
  extensions, its plot's prosperity term) and the QUARTER dial (a district whose identity is tied to a power gets its own
  term on top), both driver terms of the prosperity field with declared weights, never replacing the other drivers;
  **value, not volume** — every count, share and dial weighs each instance by its class's significance scaled by rank,
  size and patronage, ubiquitous classes low (ruling 5);
  (d‴) **the band range** — each district's value gives a floor band and a ceiling band (a row by value: the rich band
  alone at the top, middle to rich for a prosperous but not extreme district — item 71's mix —, squalid to modest for a
  poor one); every prosperity-driven state of a building inside it — the class of a new build or a re-designation, its
  condition and upkeep, its extensions, its occupants' band, its interior-ladder rung — stays inside the range; the dials
  and every building-specific amplifier move a building within the range and are clamped at it, the excess accumulating
  as pressure on the district's own value (ruling 7); item 31's floor keeps an existing shell standing while its
  condition, occupants and interior are bounded by the range; no cluster or hovel below a floor that excludes the squalid
  band;
  (d4) **the economy dial** — each sector's market share of the settlement's economy sets its amplifier in proportion,
  neutral at an even mix, no rank bonus (a 40 % sector amplifies its classes twice as strongly as a 20 % one; ruling 9),
  over a class-and-infrastructure-to-sector table (markets, warehouses and quays → trade; gates, toll houses, bridges,
  roads, walls, the customs house → taxes and tolls; workshops and guildhalls → crafts; farms, mills, granaries →
  agriculture …); a declared driver term of the field and of institution condition, significance-weighted, bounded by the
  range, its excess to pressure, feeding the vector's economic component and the identities; the map reads the make-up,
  never models the economy (item 20);
  (d5) **shared buildings partitioned** — one amplification per building, its weight toward each power or sector that
  power's standing or that sector's share relative to the others it serves, normalised to one; its amplification the
  weighted blend, never a sum; its contributions split in the same proportions (ruling 8).

### C.2 Subsection owners — one REPLACE per subsection

A REPLACE block replaces a whole heading's subsection, and most touched subsections have no sub-headings (§4.6, §4.7,
§4.11, §10.2, §13.2, §13.5, §1.4 are each one subsection), so where two seats of one part touch one subsection exactly one
seat writes it and folds the other seat's phrases into its block. A non-owner seat writes **no block** for that
subsection (an APPEND could be overwritten by the owner's REPLACE, depending on the order the assembler applies them) —
with THREE declared exceptions, where the non-owner's text is a whole new paragraph or test group too large to fold: its
APPEND TO SECTION block is applied AFTER the owner's REPLACE (the assembler's two-pass order, as the b pass re-applied its
§4.11 APPEND). Parts never share a subsection. The workflow's assembler applies block files in name order and blocks
in file order; the three exceptions hold under that order too, because each appending seat's file sorts after its
owner's (`blocks-pipeline-plots` < `blocks-pipeline-staged-…` < `blocks-pipeline-transitions-…`;
`blocks-scope-provenance-…` < `blocks-scope-render-labels-…`) — a later seat that appends to a subsection owned by a seat
whose file sorts after its own must fold instead (why item 68's placement lands in §4.7, not in C's §4.9).

### C.3 Conventions every seat follows

1. **Provenance tags** (item 62; gate (c)): a default taken from code reads `ref:⟨reference and build⟩ ⟨behaviour⟩
   (code)` followed by its P-id — for example "(provenance `ref:NG 1.2.2 per-face stop threshold (code)`, P1)". The P-id
   is the trace key into `research/findings/code-impact.md` §(d); no class, method, file or line of a reference enters the
   design or the rationale file. A value of ours keeps `own`; the owner's rule keeps `owner`.
2. **Build naming** (gate (b), R8): every sentence of the form "the reference does X" names its build — MFCG 12710731;
   VG 1.6.9 (itch 19365073); NG 1.2.2 (itch 11441280); Fantasy Manor 1.0.0 build 890; Dwellings itch 17195045; the FTG
   client of the 2026-09-25 capture. The bisector is two versions: straight-first in MFCG 12710731 and VG 1.6.9,
   bend-first in NG 1.2.2 — never "watabou's bisector".
3. **P-ids by item** (code-impact §(d)): 34 P1 · 35 P2 · 36 P3 · 37 P4, P5 · 38 P6 · 39 P7 · 40 P8 · 43 P9 · 45 P10 ·
   46 P11, P12 · 47 P13 · 53 P14 · 54 P15 · 55 P16 · 57 P17, P18 · 58 P19, P20 · 59 P21; descriptive baselines 48 D1 ·
   60 D2, D3 · 59 D4 · 61 D5; items 49 and 50 are withdrawals with no P-id; items 32, 33, 42, 44, 51, 52, 56 and 62 are
   citation or policy items with no P-id. Item 68's parity-preset cells need three rows the table lacks — proposed
   **P22** (the FTG client of the 2026-09-25 capture: one ambient and one directional light; the sun on an hour path and a
   low-intensity moon on it at night; the directional light only in clear or snow weather; shadows from a shadow map of
   extruded footprints; window lights from the server's lit set at a per-building anchor — `code-ftg-client.md` §2.2,
   §2.5.5, §2.5.6, C5, C13, C19, C22), **P23** (NG 1.2.2: per-wing prism shadows with tree and pin shadows, multiplied at
   an alpha; at night an ambience wash and a per-building radial glow by a chance — `code-neighbourhood.md` §2.11),
   **P24** (Fantasy Manor build 890: per-facet diffuse-plus-specular shading from one light; the swept-silhouette
   shadow — `code-manor.md` §2.10–§2.14, §6 rows 16–17). The pass's manager appends them to `code-impact.md` §(d) (a
   reader-tier file no seat's blocks touch) before the rendering and scope seats cite them; until then a seat writes the
   preset's behaviour with its build and the reading's section, never a P-id that does not exist.
4. **Judgment rows:** `J-A⟨item⟩.⟨n⟩`, the first digit of ⟨n⟩ the ruling's home as the amendments-b rows use it — 0 scope,
   2 model (§2), 3 kernel (§3), 4 pipeline (§4, §10, PART II, PART III), 5 §5, 6 §6, 7 §7, 8 rendering (§8, §9, §11,
   §12). One index row (the choice and the section) plus one Part A entry ("Chose X over Y because Z", Part A's table
   format) per ruling. Item numbers 32–67 cannot collide with the b rows (8–31).
5. **No heading renames.** Every seat addresses blocks by exact heading text; keep every heading line byte-identical
   (including §4.6's "the measured bent cut" and §0.2's). The banner under the title is never inside a block; the title
   block and the one-page summary take only INSERT AFTER or APPEND TO SECTION blocks (the workflow's guard).
6. **Emulation-first** (item 23, J-A23.71): where an item keeps our default over a reference's value (37's flatten under
   the MFCG preset, 43's band values, 44's enclosure), the cell carries `provenance` for the emulated base and the
   `extension` marker for ours, and the parity preset reproduces the reference; §7.6 (seat E) gains the per-preset value
   rule items 37 and 43 need.
7. **Draws and maths** (§6.1, §6.5): new draws use existing concerns except `cut.stop`; the folded bell and the mean of
   three uniforms are uniform-only helpers; every power, exponential or logarithm goes through `dmath` (§6.5 (3)).
8. **Document checks** (§17.1 (2)): the code readings and `code-impact.md` are in the C3 corpus, so the assembler runs
   the three-copy method (untouched / mine / combined) and a seat rewords a hit rather than allow-listing it. Expected
   changed top-level keys (for `--sections`): the summary, 0.2, 0.3, 1.3, 1.4, 2.5, 2.6, 3.1, 3.4, 3.5, 4.2, 4.4, 4.5,
   4.6, 4.7, 4.8, 4.9, 5.1, 5.2, 6.1, 6.5, 7.1, 7.6, 8.2, 8.3, 8.4, 8.10, 9.3, 10.2, 13.2, 13.3, 13.5, 15.1, 16, 17.1,
   II.2, II.3, II.5, PART III, Delegated decisions, Judgment calls, Staged by, Appendix A — and, for items 63–67, 1.1, 2.1,
   2.2, 2.3, 2.7, 2.8, 2.9, 3.7, 3.10, 4.11, 5.3, 6.2, 6.3, 7.3, 7.4, 7.7, 8.5, 8.6, 9.1, 9.2, 10.4, 11.1, 11.2, 11.3,
   11.4, 14, 15.2, II.6, II.7 (II.7.3, II.7.4 and the new II.7.5, II.7.6) — and, for item 68, 1.2, 1.7, 7.8, 8.1, 8.8,
   8.9, 9.4, 10.3, 12.5, 12.7, 12.8 and 13.4 besides keys already listed (2.7, 2.8, 2.9, 4.2, 4.7, 4.11, 5.2, 5.3, 6.1,
   6.2, 6.5, 7.1, 7.4, 7.6, 8.2, 8.4, 8.5, 8.10, 11.1–11.3, 13.2, 13.3, 13.5, 14, 15.1, 15.2, 16, 0.2, the summary, II.6,
   II.7, Delegated decisions, Judgment calls, Staged by).
9. **No deferral wording.** A held part is an evidence-gate wait for Fable's retrovalidation, recorded in the seat's
   report (§C.4), never "TBD" or "later" in the design; nothing in the design depends on a held part.

10. **Owner-rule items (63–68).** They carry no evidence gate, no build naming and no P-id — except item 68's emulation
    note, whose reference facts take the three gates (§B item 68: build names, P22–P24, the held MFCG clause): the owner's words are the
    authority and the item's lettered consequences the program's vetoable interpretation. Seats design them as the
    amendments-b pass designed items 8–31 — records typed with units, stage steps with inputs, outputs and failure
    handling, rule tables with their context vector, interfaces, and the `T⟨item⟩-⟨n⟩` bars — and judge them by owner
    fidelity (each §B section's fidelity line). Cells of the owner's rule carry provenance `owner`; our defaults `own`;
    every row the references lack carries the `extension` marker, so the parity presets switch it off (item 23,
    J-A23.71) — none of 63–67 has a reference behaviour to emulate; item 68 has (FTG's day and night light, NG's prism
    pass, FM's facet shading), so its preset cells carry `ref:… (code)` provenance and the default cells the owner's rule. A "recommended" ruling in §B is the route's
    reading (PLAUSIBLE): the home editor takes it or rules otherwise, and records the ruling as `J-A⟨item⟩.⟨n⟩` either way.
    The owner's standing rules outrank an item's interpretation; where one would contradict them (none found; the
    nearest are item 67's material-keyed durations against the owner's materials rule, and item 65's undercity streets
    against item 12's "no sewer alleys"), the §B section names the conflict and the ruling it needs. Item 68 meets no
    standing owner rule (the 2026-09-25 rule — shadows that use the height number — it fulfils); it meets two owner-adopted
    DEFAULTS, the ink look (J10, D-1) and emulation-first (item 23), in its default-shadow question (ruling 2 of §B item
    68), and the design's own drawing-path decision (J-R4, D-1, VIS-7) in its GPU clause (ruling 1).

20, 22) receives an item. Where a c-item meets a b-item's text: 37, 43 and 44 meet item 23's emulation marker (§7.6);
42 meets items 30 and 31 (courts, hidden plots, the geometry floor); 47 meets item 24 (a `market` district per market);
50 meets item 29 (its home, §2.6) and 23; 57 meets item 26 (`extent`, farm modes); 59 and 60 meet items 28 and 29 (the
stage-3 hook, the plan rung, entrances and basements); 32 and 62 meet item 21 (the licence ruling, J-A21.01).

access record the closure creates), 63 ↔ 66 (a front requiring a concealed door, (d)), 65 ↔ 67 (the buried old town from

#### C.9.2 FINDING — the assembler's one-pass order erases an APPEND that precedes a REPLACE of the same heading

The assembler prompt applies "files in name order and blocks in file order" and merges a SECOND REPLACE of one heading
three-way (J87) — but an APPEND TO SECTION or INSERT AFTER applied before a later file's REPLACE of the same heading is
silently erased by that REPLACE. §C.2's exceptions were written on the premise that each appending seat's file sorts
after its owner's; **exception 4 does not**: T's APPEND on §4.5 (item 72's paragraphs "Neighbourhood character,
identity, the three dials and the band range") would be applied BEFORE C's REPLACE of §4.5, because
`blocks-pipeline-transitions-…` sorts before `blocks-pipeline-walls-districts-hinterland` ('t' < 'w') — the claim in §C.2
that it "sorts after" is wrong. C's §4.5 REPLACE has landed (its block file, l. 704), so under the one-pass order item
72's home text would be lost at assembly. This route sends no d or e text to a C-owned subsection (§4.2, §4.4, §4.5,
§4.8, §4.9, II.1) and no E2 text to E1's §7.7, so exception 4 is the one known case — but any editor who, under the
bundle's double-REPLACE guard, APPENDs to a subsection a later-sorting file REPLACEs meets the same loss.

**Recommended fix (the manager's, in `workflows/12`'s assembler prompt, before `assemble-r0` runs):** "apply every
REPLACE block first — files in name order, blocks in file order, a second REPLACE of one heading merged three-way as now
— then every INSERT AFTER and APPEND TO SECTION block in the same order; J-ROWS and RATIONALE-A as now." Under that
order every exception of §C.2 and §C.9.3 holds whatever the file names, and an INSERT AFTER lands under the heading of
the replaced subsection. Without it: exception 4's paragraphs must be recovered by the verify and repair rounds (the
part verifier will find item 72's home text missing), a worse path. (Not changed by this seat: the workflow script is
the manager's.)
